RAJYA SABHA 
Tuesday, the 3rd February, 2026/14 Magha, 1947 (Saka) 
The House met at eleven of the clock, 
MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair. 
OBITUARY REFERENCE 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Hon. Members, it is with profound sorrow, I inform the House about 
the passing away of Shrimati Sushila Tiriya, former Member of the Rajya Sabha on 1st 
February, 2026, at the age of 69 years. She represented  the State of Odisha from  2nd 
July, 1986 to 1st July, 1992 and again from 4th April, 2006 to 3rd April, 2012. She also 
served as a Member of the Tenth and the Eleventh Lok Sabha. 
B orn on 6th February, 1956 at Kalua-khaman  in district Mayurbhanj of Odisha, 
Shrimati Sushila Tiriya, pursued her education at Utkal University. From an early age, 
she was deeply committed to social service, particularly, for the upliftment and 
empowerment of tribal communities.  She organised tribal festivals and worked for 
safeguarding the rich traditional culture of various tribes, development of rural and tribal 
areas, and encouraging young artistes and sportspersons. 
I n the passing away of Shrimati Sushila Tiriya , the nation has lost an 
experienced legislator and a distinguished social worker. We deeply mourn the passing 
away of Shrimati Sushila Tiriya. 
 I request Members to rise in their places and observe silence as a mark of 
respect to the memory of the departed. 
(Hon. Members then stood in silence for one minute.) 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Secretary-General will convey to the members of the bereaved family 
our sense of profound sorrow and deep sympathy. 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Now, Papers to be laid on the Table.

Demand for including the Kothavalasa-Kirandul line in the proposed Visakhapatnam 
division of New South Coast Railway Zone 
SHRI GOLLA BABURAO (Andhra Pradesh) :  Mr. Chairman, Sir, in pursuance of the 
Andhra Pradesh (Reorganisation) Act, the Ministry of Railways is taking steps to create 
new Railway Zone in AP with Visakhapatnam as its Zonal Headquarters. Hon. PM also 
laid the foundation-stone for its headquarters, allocated money for the same last year 
and the Union Cabinet has also approved DPR for this Zone.  It is an assurance pending 
of more than 12 years and I hope that, at least, the new Zone would be notified at the 
earliest, and they would complete the construction of headquarters and provide other 
infrastructure as soon as possible to make it functional. 
 Sir, the new Zone has four divisions – Guntakal, Guntur, Vijayawada and 
Visakhapatnam.  And, all these divisions have their respective jurisdiction with different 
sections. But, Sir, I am given to understand that the Ministry of Railways is proposing 
to transfer Kothavalasa-Kirandul line from Visakhapatnam Division to Rayagada 
Division of East Coast Railway Zone, instead of keeping it within South Coast Railway 
Zone since the above line falls within the Visakhapatnam Division.  Not only this, 
Palasa-Ichchapuram sections are also proposed to transfer to new Rayagada Division 
against the wishes of the people of AP in general and North coastal AP in particular.  If 
these sections are transferred to Rayagada Division, the proposed new South Coast 
Zone becomes a mere skeleton, as this Zone is going to lose huge revenue and its 
existence would be in jeopardy. The proposed unscientific move structurally weakens 
not only Visakhapatnam Division, but also the new Zone and impacts its financial self-
sufficiency.   
In view of the above, I demand for inclusion of Kothavalasa-Kirandul and other 
lines taken to Rayagada Division be included in the Visakhapatnam Division only and 
also demand for early notification of the new South Coast Railway Zone.  Thank you. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: The following hon. Members associated themselves with the matter 
raised by hon. Member, Shri Golla Baburao: Shri Javed Ali Khan (Uttar Pradesh), Dr. 
Sasmit Patra (Odisha), Shri R. Girirajan (Tamil Nadu), Shri Niranjan Bishi (Odisha), 
Shrimati Sulata Deo (Odisha), Shri Meda Raghunadha Reddy (Andhra Pradesh), Shri

Concern over the coastal erosion and climate displacement at India’s eastern 
coastline 
SHRI A.D. SINGH (Bihar) :  Thank you, Mr. Chairman, Sir, for giving me this 
opportunity to speak.  I rise to draw the attention of this august House to a silent but 
devastating humanitarian crisis unfolding along India's eastern coastline - coastal 
erosion and climate displacement.  As reported recently, 46 villages across 
Kendrapara, Puri and Ganjam districts of Odisha have been severely affected by coastal 
erosion.  Entire habitations like Satabhaya have been wiped off the map by the 
advancing sea.  Families, who once lived with dignity as farmers and fishers, have been 
uprooted, resettled inland and reduced to daily-wage labourers.  Safety has been 
provided to them, yes - but livelihoods have not been provided.  People relocated to 
colonies like Bagapatia live far from the sea, away from fishing grounds, agricultural 
land and markets.  Young students have dropped out of school; elderly persons are 
dependent; and women are forced into insecure work.  These are not statistics; these 
are India's first climate refugees.  
The National Centre for Coastal Research has warned that over one-third of 
India's 7,000 km coastline is eroding, with Odisha among the worst affected.  In West 
Bengal, 60 per cent of the coastline along the Digha-Shankarpur and Sundarbans is 
facing the threat of erosion.  In Tamil Nadu, 43 per cent, in Andhra Pradesh, 26 per 
cent and in Puducherry, 56 per cent of the coastline is under threat.  Rising sea levels, 
cyclones, sand mining, destruction of mangroves, ports and embankments - all 
aggravated by unplanned development - are accelerating this crisis.  If this continues, 
thousands more villages will follow Satabhaya.   
I urge the Government to act on five fronts: first, declare climate displacement a 
national policy concern; second, ensure livelihood-linked rehabilitation and not just 
housing; third, restore mangroves and natural barriers instead of relying only on 
concrete walls; fourth, strictly regulate sand mining and coastal construction; and 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
19

 
 
finally, create a national climate refugee framework with dedicated funding.  The sea is 
not just eroding land; it is eroding lives.  If the Government does not act today, history 
will ask us: when India's coastal poor were drowning silently, where was the nation's 
conscience?   Thank you, Sir. 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  The following hon. Members associated themselves with the matter 
raised by the hon . Member, Shri A.D. Singh : Shrimati Priyanka Chaturvedi 
(Maharashtra), Shri Javed Ali Khan (Uttar Pradesh), Dr. Fauzia Khan (Maharashtra),

Concern over the impact of United States' tariff policies on Indian exports and 
domestic industry 
SHRI MASTHAN RAO YADAV BEEDHA (Andhra Pradesh) :   Sir, I wish to draw the 
attention of this august House to the impact of United States’ tariff policies on Indian 
exports, while also placing on record the important and positive developments 
announced on 2 nd February 2026 as well as the issues that still require clarity and 
sustained engagement.  During 2025, the United States imposed a 25 per cent 
reciprocal tariff on Indian goods, followed by an additional 25 per cent punitive duty 
linked to India's purchases of Russian oil. …(Interruptions)…  Let me complete. 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Please don’t interfere.  Let him speak. 
SHRI MASTHAN RAO YADAV BEEDHA:  As a result, several Indian exports faced an 
effective tariff burden of 50-58 per cent, impacting nearly USD 50 billion worth of 
shipments to the U.S. market.  These measures severely disrupted trade flows, eroded 
competitiveness and created uncertainty for exporters, particularly MSMEs and labour-
intensive sectors. 
In this context, I would like to place on record my appreciation of the determined 
efforts of the hon . Prime Minister of India, Shri Narendra Modi ji, and the NDA 
Government as well as the Commerce Ministry whose proactive diplomatic 
engagement with the United States has yielded tangible results. The announcement on 
2nd February 2026 of an understanding to reduce the reciprocal tariff from 25 per cent 
to 18 per cent is a significant relief for Indian exporters and a strong signal of India's 
growing stature in global trade negotiations. The positive market response, including 
the strengthening of the Indian rupee, reflects confidence in this leadership-driven 
26
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
 
engagement.  At the same time, it is important to flag a crucial area of uncertainty.  The 
announcement so far is based on public statements and oral communication, and 
formal notification is yet to be issued under the U.S. law, including under the 
International Emergency Economic Powers Act.  Notably, President Trump's statement 
refers specifically to the reciprocal tariff and does not explicitly confirm the withdrawal 
of the additional 25 per cent punitive duty.  This distinction is evident from the U.S. 
customs entry documents, including shrimp import filings, which show two separate 
duty codes - one for the reciprocal tariff and another for the Russia-India punitive levy 
- each levied independently.  Until a formal legal clarification is issued, exporters 
continue to face uncertainty.  The shrimp industry illustrates this clearly.  Indian shrimp 
exports to the U.S. declined by around 26 per cent in late 2025 due to high tariffs.  While 
the reduction to 18 per cent restores competitiveness and offers immediate relief, a full 
revival depends on the complete removal of the punitive duty.  I, therefore, appeal to 
the Government to continue and intensify dialogue with the U.S. Administration to 
secure formal withdrawal of the punitive tariff.

raised by the hon. Member, Shri Masthan Rao Yadav Beedha:  Dr. Fauzia Khan

(Rajasthan), Dr. Sasmit Patra (Odisha), Shri Prakash Chik Baraik (West Bengal), Shri

Concern over the growing drug menace in the country 
SHRI JAGGESH (Karnataka): Sir, my subject is concern over growing drug menace 
threatening India's youth. The escalating drug violence in our country poses a grave 
threat to public health, public safety and the future of our youth. Across India, drug 
abuse and trafficking are spreading rapidly, particularly among students and young 
professionals. Synthetic drugs are increasingly circulated through organized inter-State 
and international networks, exploiting urban centres and digital platforms. Within this 
national crisis, Bangalore stands out as a particular concerning example. As a major 
metropolitan city and a technology hub, it has witnessed a sharp rise in drug 
consumption and trafficking. Educational institutions, workplaces, entertainment hubs 
and residential areas have become prime targets for drug syndicates, highlighting the 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
27

 
 
urgent need for stronger intelligence, preventive policy and coordinated enforcement. 
The impact of drug abuse goes far beyond the individual. It devastates family, leads to 
financial hardships, domestic conflicts and lasting emotional trauma. It fuels crime, 
mental illness, road accidents, workplace inefficiency and broader social instability. 
Despite this risk, conviction rates remain low, exposing gaps in investigation and 
prosecution while rehabilitation, counselling and awareness programmes remain 
insufficient. Effectiveness of our national response is further weakened when certain 
State Governments, including Karnataka, fail to demonstrate sustained commitment 
and seriousness in tackling this menace. A comprehensive and decisive approach is 
required, combining strict enforcement with widespread awareness, early intervention 
and accessible de-addiction rehabilitation.  
I urge the Government to spearhead a strong national movement against drug 
abuse and trafficking with coordinated action, strict enforcement and robust support 
system to safeguard our youth, protect families and strengthen the society as whole. 
Thank you.

public programmes in many States 
SHRI DEBASHISH SAMANTARAY (Odisha) :  Sir, the matter I am bringing to your 
notice concerns each and every one of us in this House.  Whenever the Prime Minister, 
President, Vice President or Cabinet Ministers visit our constituencies, as MPs, we are 
invited and we are also there on the podium or on the stage.  Recently, the Vice 
President of India, our Chairman, the protector of this House, had visited Cuttack for 
Netaji’s Jayanti.  In that function, Sir, you went on to inaugurate a building, a memorial 
of Netaji where, when the foundation was laid by Naveen Patnaik ji, I was the MLA of 
Cuttack, and now as Rajya Sabha, MP, my nodal district is Cuttack.   
Sir, the Collector, Cuttack, did not invite me to the function; he did not even 
have my name.  There was Lok Sabha MP, there were all the local MLAs from BJD, 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]

be given to the hon. Members of this House whenever such occasions are held.  The

Concern over the unregulated access of children to social media and smartphones 
SHRI SANJAY KUMAR JHA (Bihar): Sir, I rise to draw the urgent attention of this House 
to the unregulated exposure of children to social media. In 2025, Australia enacted a 
law prohibiting under-16s from major platforms after a study showed 96 per cent of 
children aged 10-15 use social media and seven in ten encountered harmful content.  
In India, a 2025 Local Circles study found that 82 per cent of children aged 14-
16 can operate smart phones, only 57 per cent use them mainly for education and 76 
per cent access social media research. Research indicates 11-37 per cent show 
problematic use, causing language and communication setbacks, social and emotional 
learning delays, poor motor development, disrupted sleep and reduced focus. 
As countries worldwide evolve policy to regulate children’s exposure to social 
media, I strongly support deliberation in this House to curb excessive screen time 
among toddlers, pre-teenagers and teenagers. This is because the current literature 
on screen-time exposure and child development broadly suggests that excessive 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
33

 
 
screen use in early childhood is negatively associated with developmental outcome 
among children. While digital classrooms were unavoidable during pandemic, parents 
and school administration must actively monitor duration and content quality.  
According to the Indian Academy of Pediatrics Guidelines on Screen Time Among 
Children, very early exposure to screen-based media and excessive screen time seems 
to be widely prevalent among Indian children. Also, a new study published based on 
the survey of 3,624 parents across five Indian States found that screen exposure among 
children aged two to five years far exceeds the World Health Organization guidelines. 
Such findings underscore the need for timely safeguard to protect development 
outcome.  To begin with, smart phone being ubiquitous device in Indian households 
with over 85 per cent of Indian households owning smart phones, their use by children 
merit regulation. The House may consider, specifically, debating whether smart phone 
packs would carry a statutory use advisory. The Economic Survey 2025-2026 has 
flagged a rapid rise of digital addiction and screen-related mental health problem as a 
major healthcare issue, particularly, among children and adolescents. A timely 
intervention to safeguard the developmental milestone of India’s adolescents is 
essential to leverage India’s demographic dividend in realizing the goals of Viksit Bharat 
2047. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: The following hon. Members associated themselves with the matter 
raised by the hon. Member, Shri Sanjay Kumar Jha: Shrimati Rajathi (Tamil Nadu), Shri 
Madan Rathore (Rajasthan), Dr. John Brittas (Kerala), Shri Niranjan Bishi (Odisha), 
Dr. Kanimozhi NVN Somu (Tamil Nadu), Shri Gurwinder Singh Oberoi (Jammu  and  
Kashmir), Shri Manan Kumar Mishra (Bihar), Shrimati Sudha Murty (Nominated), Shri

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Now, Question Hour.  Q. No. 31     
 
ORAL ANSWERS TO QUESTIONS  
International expansion of UPI 
*31. DR. MEDHA VISHRAM KULKARNI: Will the Minister of FINANCE be pleased to 
state: 
(a) the number of countries where the Unified Payments Interface (UPI) is currently 
operational or linked with local payment systems; 
(b) the total transaction value and volume carried out through international UPI 
linkages during the last three years, year-wise:  
(c) whether Government has conducted any assessment of UPI's role in enhancing 
India's digital payment leadership globally; and  
(d) the future roadmap for expanding UPI to additional countries, particularly for the 
benefit of Indian diaspora and international trade? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF FINANCE (SHRI PANKAJ 
CHAUDHARY): (a) to (d): A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
38
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
 
Statement 
(a) and (b) Unified Payment Interface (UPI) is currently working in 8 countries 
i.e. Bhutan, France, Mauritius, Nepal, Qatar, Singapore, Sri Lanka and UAE. The year-
wise transactions (both value and volume) reported during the last three financial years 
are as under: 
Financial Year Volume (Actuals) Value ( ₹Lakhs) 
2023-24              37,060            1,970 
2024-25            755,445        25,853  
2025-26*          1,486,537        33,043  
*Till December 2025 
(c) and (d)  The Government, Reserve Bank of India (RBI) and NPCI 
International Payments Ltd. (NIPL) have been taking up various steps for international 
expansion of Unified Payment Interface (UPI). These inter-alia, include 
operationalisation of UPI linkages with comparable fast payment systems of partner 
countries to facilitate cross ‑border person ‑to‑person (P2P) remittance flows, as well 
as the establishment of travel ‑based corridors to enable cross ‑border 
person‑to‑merchant (P2M) payments. Further, NIPL is offering the UPI technology 
stack to interested partner countries for development of sovereign, real-time digital 
payment systems similar to UPI. 
The International Monetary Fund (IMF) report on ‘Growing Retail Digital 
Payments (The Value of Interoperability)’ dated June 2025 has recognized UPI as the 
world’s largest retail fast-payment system by transaction volume. Further, as per ACI 
Worldwide report 2024, UPI accounts for around 49% of transaction volumeof the 
global real-time retail payments. 
MR. CHAIRMAN:   First supplementary – Dr. Medha Vishram Kulkarni.

*32. [The questioner was absent.] 
Adverse effects of excessive salt consumption 
*32. SHRI MUKUL BALKRISHNA WASNIK : Will the MINISTER OF HEALTH AND 
FAMILY WELFARE be pleased to state: 
(a)   whether excessive salt consumption has emerged as a critical health crisis in 
India, primarily driving a 'silent pandemic' of Hypertension and Cardiovascular 
diseases; 
42
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
 
(b)    if so, the details thereof and steps taken by Government to promote awareness 
about the ill-effects of excessive salt consumption ; 
(c)    whether it is a fact that consumption of Low Sodium Salt Substitutes (LSS) can 
go a long way in combating this 'silent pandemic' ; and 
(d)   if so, the action taken by Government to ensure accessibility and affordability of 
LSSs? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF HEALTH AND FAMILY  
WELFARE (SHRI PRATAPRAO JADHAV): (a) to (d) A Statement is laid on the 
Table of the House.  
Statement  
(a) to (d) As per Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR), excessive salt 
consumption is a contributor to the growing burden of hypertension and cardiovascular 
diseases.   
The Government of India, through the Food Safety and Standards Authority of India 
(FSSAI), is implementing the  Eat Right India  movement to promote healthy eating 
practices and awareness about the harmful effects of excessive salt consumption. 
Under this movement, key activities include: 
   Mass media and social media campaigns such as “Aaj Se ThodaKam”  to 
encourage gradual reduction in salt, sugar and fat intake in daily diet.  
   Development and dissemination of the Eat Right Toolkit  for frontline health 
workers to counsel communities on limiting high fat, sugar and salt (HFSS) foods.  
Based on a review of the available evidence on effects and safety, and consideration of 
additional contextual factors, WHO in 2025 recommended: 
  To reduce blood pressure and the risk of cardiovascular diseases, it is 
recommended to reduce sodium intake to less than 2 gm/day (strong 
recommendation). 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
43

 
 
   In this context, using less regular table salt is an important part of an overall 
sodium reduction strategy. If choosing to use table salt, WHO suggests replacing 
regular table salt with lower-sodium substitutes that contain potassium (conditional 
recommendation). This recommendation is intended for adults (not pregnant women 
or children) in general populations, excluding individuals with kidney impairments or 
with other circumstances or conditions that might compromise potassium excretion. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Third supplementary, Dr. Parmar Jashvantsinh Salamsinh. 
Z< .q=6<= Pi;E:CAह A<Q6CAह: OI, K‘Oh Tq cdeS KS Nh fNh vdOI cl ·h Oh TWJ ह‘,

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Question No. 33. 
 Setting up of more Ayurvedic Research Centres in Bihar 
*33. SHRIMATI DHARMSHILA GUPTA: Will the Minister of AYUSH be pleased to 
state: 
(a)    whether Government proposes to set up another Ayurvedic Research Centre in  
the State of Bihar to promote Ayurveda; 
(b)    if so, the details thereof; 
(c)   the details of Ayurvedic Research Centres functioning in the country including    
Bihar, State-wise; and 
(d)    the details of funds allocated and utilized for Research and Development in the 
said Centres during the last three years and the current year? 
 
THE MINISTER OF STATE OF THE MINISTRY OF AYUSH (SHRI PRATAPRAO 
JADHAV): (a) to (d) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
46
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
 
Statement 
(a) and (b) There is no such proposal under consideration at present. 
(c) The Ministry of Ayush, Government of India has set up Central Council for 
Research in Ayurvedic Sciences (CCRAS), an autonomous organization for 
undertaking, coordinating, formulating, developing, and promoting research on 
scientific lines in Ayurvedic sciences. The research activities are carried out through its 
30 Institutes/Centres located all over India and through collaborative studies with 
various Universities, Hospitals, and Institutes. A State-wise list of Ayurvedic Research 
Institutes / Centresfunctioning in the country including Bihar is enclosed as  
Annexure-I. 
(d) Details of funds allocated and utilised for all the activities including 
research and developmentof the said centres during the last three years and the current 
year(till 28.01.2026) are placed at Annexure-II.

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Now, Question No.34. 
Ayush Nivesh Saarthi 
*34. SHRIMATI SEEMA DWIVEDI: Will the Minister of AYUSH be pleased to state; 
 (a)    the outcomes achieved in facilitating investment through platforms such as 
AYUSH Invest Saarthi; 
(b)    he details of operational projects, participation of micro, small and medium 
enterprise (MSMEs) and employment generation thereunder; 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
55

 
 
(c)    the major countries contributing to foreign direct investment (FDI) and research 
and development (R and D) collaboration in the AYUSH sector; 
(d)     whether there has been an increase in exports of AYUSH medicines and products 
under the AYUSH Invest Saarthi Platform; and  
(e)     if so, the details of the major countries importing AYUSH medicines and products 
under the said platform?  
THE MINISTER OF STATE OF THE MINISTRY OF AYUSH  (SHRI PRATAPRAO 
JADHAV): (a) to (e) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) Ministry of Ayush has launched Ayush Nivesh Saarthi portal on 29th May 
2025 which aims to boost traditional medicine systems as a strong economic sector of 
the country. The portal has acted as a comprehensive investment facilitation platform, 
promoting opportunities in Ayush sector. It serves as a global hub for information 
related to trade in medicinal plants, derivatives and extracts and supports the growing 
export market for Ayush products encouraging research and development as well as 
collaborations between industry and academia. 
Through Ayush Nivesh Saarthi Portal, Ministry of Ayush has consolidated 
existing policy frameworks and incentive structures, showcased investment ready 
projects and facilitated real time investor engagement under one unified interface. 
(b) The Ayush Nivesh Saarthi Portal is primarily an investment facilitation and 
information dissemination platform, while it does not directly implement or monitor 
operational projects, the portal provides information and guidance to a wide range of 
stakeholders, including Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs), by 
showcasing investment opportunities, relevant policies, schemes and Ayush Statistics. 
(c) Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) up to 100% is permitted through the 
automatic route in the Ayush sector, encouraging global investment. Majorly FDI in 
Ayush sector has been received from countries like Mauritius, Singapore, France and 
Malaysia. 
56
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
 
Ministry of Ayush has signed 27 Country to Country MoUs for Cooperation in 
field of Traditional Medicine, 54 Institute to Institute level MoUs with foreign institutes 
for undertaking Collaborative Research/Academic collaboration. The details are 
attached at Annexure-A and Annexure-B respectively.  
(e) and (e) The Ayush Nivesh Saarthi Portal serves as an investment facilitation and 
trade promotion platform. While the portal itself does not directly track or attribute 
export figures to individual transactions, its launch has strengthened visibility of the 
Ayush sector in global markets and supported exporters through dissemination of 
country-wise market intelligence, standards, and incentive schemes. Major importing 
countries of Ayush medicines and products, include the United States of America, 
European Union countries, United Arab Emirates, France, Russia, UK, and South-East 
Asian nations as per data available withDirectorate General of Commercial Intelligence 
and Statistics (DGCI and S), Ministry of Commerce and Industry.

Now, Question No. 35. 
Regulating prices of pharmaceutical drugs 
35. MS. SWATI MALIWAL : Will the Minister of CHEMICALS AND FERTILIZERS be 
pleased to state: 
(a)   whether any consultations or policy reviews are underway for capping or regulating 
prices of pharmaceutical drugs, particularly high-cost and life-saving medicines, 
beyond those covered under National List of Essential Medicines;  
(b)  whether National Pharmaceutical Pricing Authority has examined or recommended 
expansion of price controls, trade margin rationalisation or other regulatory 
measures for non-scheduled drugs and emerging high-cost therapies;  
(c)     whether representations have been received from public health experts, patient 
groups, committees or State Governments seeking wider drug price regulation 
and action taken thereon; and 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
71

 
 
(d)     whether Government proposes to review Drugs (Prices Control) Order, 2013, to 
strengthen affordability? 
THE MINISTER IN THE MINISTRY OF CHEMICALS AND FERTILIZERS (SHRI JAGAT 
PRAKASH NADDA: (a) to (d): A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) to (c) Consultations on policy issues are an ongoing and transparent activity 
which is undertaken by the Department to assess the relevance, impact and efficiency 
of the existing policy reform required, if any, keeping in view the interest of all 
stakeholders.  The Department and National Pharmaceutical Pricing Authority (NPPA) 
has held consultation with various stakeholder with pharmaceutical industry 
associations like CII, FICCI, OPPI, IPA, AIMED, ADVAMED, MTAI, USIBC, 
ASSOCHAM, NATHEALTH etc., MSMEs Association like LaghuUdhyog Bharti, IDMA, 
FOPE, Patient advocacy groups like AIDAN, MSF, Patient Safety and access initiative 
of India Foundation. etc., on the matters concerning National Pharmaceuticals Pricing 
Policy, 2012 (NPPP, 2012) and Drug (Prices Control) Order, 2013 (DPCO, 2013).  
Feedbacks were received from the stakeholders on the operation of the existing policy 
framework. These suggestions/ feedbacks received during such consultations provide 
stakeholders input on issues requiring redressal in the existing policy, if any. The 
Department and NPPA remain engaged with stakeholders with a view to understanding 
issues and resolving them either under extant mechanism or by making appropriate 
provision. 
(d) Review of DPCO, 2013 is a continuous process which is done periodically 
by the Department or as and when required to ensure that the provisions of DPCO, 
2013 are enforced effectively so that the essential drugs are made accessible and 
available to the public at affordable price.

MR. CHAIRMAN: Fourth supplementary; Dr. M. Thambidurai.  
DR. M. THAMBIDURAI: Sir, the question is regarding regulating prices, particularly of 
high cost and life-saving medicines. In Tamil Nadu, during our Amma Jayalalitha 
period, we started opening Amma clinics and Amma pharmacy to give medicines to 
poor people at reasonable and affordable price. We did that. But, after the DMK 
Government came to power, they closed all Amma clinics and Amma pharmacy. Their 
attitude is not to help patients and poor people. This is the attitude of the DMK 
Government which is now running the State and spoiling the whole system. So, I want 
to know one thing, because this is about price control, especially giving affordability to 
the poor is very, very important. Even the hon. Prime Minister also started many 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
75

 
 
projects and many pharmacies are opened for giving medicines to poor people. At the 
same time, I say, in the permitted practice of producing medicines, cost is also 
involved. Cost may be high. I can accept that. But, at the same time, we have to 
provide for price control and make it affordable to common man. We have come to 
know that many pharmacies are selling expired medicines; innocent people are buying 
such kind of medicines. I wish to know whether the Government has a policy on price 
control and also on expired medicines to avoid this kind of thing. Secondly, will the 
Government of India direct the State Government of Tamil Nadu to see that the clinics 
and pharmacies, which were established in the name of Amma Jayalalitha, are revived? 
Would you direct the State Government? I want to know that.  
SHRI JAGAT PRAKASH NADDA: Sir, as far as giving direction to the State Government 
is concerned, actually, it is within the State Government's jurisdiction; they have to 
decide about it. But, the Government of India has taken many initiatives of which Tamil 
Nadu State is also taking the benefit, that is, free drugs and diagnostic facilities. We 
are giving financial support through National Health Mission to district hospitals, 
Community Health Centres, Primary Health Centres and Sub-Centres. There is a 
particular number of medicines and diagnostic facilities for each facility. For example, 
for Sub-Centres, we have got a number of medicines which are given free. For Primary 
Health Centres, there is a different number where we give those drugs free. For 
Community Health Centres, the number is more; for investigation also, the number is 
more. In the same way, it is for the district hospital. So, free drugs and diagnostic 
facility is being provided under NHM. We have got one more programme called the 
Pradhan Mantri Jan Aushadhi Yojana. In this programme, we are giving generic 
medicines. They are sold at 50 to 80 per cent cheaper than the market rate. They are 
generic medicines. There are about 18,000 Jan Aushadi Kendras through which we are 
trying to see that patients get benefit out of it. Then, for secondary and tertiary 
healthcare, we also have the programme called as PMJAY -- Pradhan Mantri Jan 
Aarogya Yojana. In that also, during hospitalization, cashless treatment is given to 
them, and free drugs and diagnostic facilities are also given to them. We have got one 
more programme called AMRIT -- Affordable Medicines and Reliable Implants for 
Treatment. In this, we sell branded medicines at 50 per cent less than the market rate. 
That is how we are trying to do it. Then, as I said, free drugs and diagnostic facilities 
are being provided to various Government facilities of the country. I would like to share 
76
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
 
with you here that the out of pocket expenditure in the last ten years has reduced from 
62.6 per cent to 39.4 per cent.  So, you can understand how we have tried to see to it 
that the common man is benefited out of it and so from 62.6 per cent,  it has now come 
down to 39.4 per cent.

[ 3 February, 2026 ]
77

 
 
Home-based physiotherapy services for paralysis/stroke patients 
#*36 SHRIMATI DARSHANA SINGH : Will the MINISTER OF HEALTH AND FAMILY 
WELFARE be pleased to state: 
(a)     whether access to regular physiotherapy treatment for patients suffering from 
stroke or paralysis is limited in the country, particularly in rural and remote 
areas; 
(b)     whether any special programme or financial assistance scheme is proposed to 
provide home-based treatment to stroke/paralysis patients and to improve 
access to health care services in future, if so, the details thereof; and 
(c)  whether there is any plan to enhance the availability of home-physiotherapy 
equipment such as tools, stretching bands and mobile rehabilitation kits 
through Public Private Partnership? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF HEALTH AND FAMILY  
WELFARE (SHRI PRATAPRAO JADHAV): (a) to (c) A Statement is laid on the Table 
of the House.  
Statement  
(a) Health is a State Subject. The Department of Health and Family Welfare, 
Government of India provides technical and financial support to the States and Union 
Territories under the National Health Mission (NHM) to strengthen public health care 
system.  
Under the National Programme for Health Care of the Elderly (NPHCE), 
rehabilitation services, including physiotherapy, are provided for elderly patients with 
functional limitations, including those suffering from stroke or paralysis.  
 
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi.  
78
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
 
 (b) Financial assistance is provided to States/UTs for implementation of 
services including physiotherapy under NPHCE as per approved Programme 
Implementation Plans (PIPs). 
Further, the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare has implemented the National 
Programme for Palliative Care (NPPC) under the National Health Mission since 2013 to 
support patients with chronic and debilitating condition, including stroke and paralysis 
patients. 
(c)  The State Governments are given technical and financial support under 
NHM to enhance the availability of physiotherapy services. Since health is a State 
Subject, the mode of implementation is decided by the State/UT Governments.  
MR. CHAIRMAN:  First supplementary, Shrimati Darshana Singh,

MR. DEPUTY CHAIRMAN: Shri Ritabrata Banerjee; you have 16 minutes. 
SHRI RITABRATA BANERJEE (West Bengal) :  Mr. Deputy Chairman Sir, rising to 
speak on behalf of my party, the All India Trinamool Congress, on the hon. President’s 
Address, I wish to begin by stating that we live in the world’s largest democracy. The 
roots of our democratic system are extremely ancient—dating back to between the 
sixth and fourth centuries BCE—when there were sixteen Mahajanapadas in our 
country. Among these Mahajanapadas were States like Vriji and Malla, which 
functioned as republics, as forms of popular governance. 
 Today, our country is the world’s largest democracy. Since the first general 
election of 1952, people have decided through their votes who will govern. But 
unfortunately, a situation has emerged where the rulers want to decide who will vote 
and who will not.*

SHRI RITABRATA BANERJEE : * Now Sir, the hon. President spoke about Vande 
Bharat trains. We were happy to hear that a Vande Bharat would run from Howrah to 
Kamakhya. But suddenly someone   noticed   that   while    booking    tickets,    only 
vegetarian food options were available.  In our State, Sir, 98.7% of people are non-
vegetarian. In Assam, where the train is headed, 78.6% of the people consume non-
vegetarian food. Can the Government decide what people should eat and what they 
should not? On Republic Day, attempts were made to interfere with people’s food 
habits. In one district, an order was issued banning non-vegetarian food. Who will eat 
what, who will wear what, who will speak which language—these are all attempts to 
impose authoritarianism and fascism. 
Sir, the President’s Address mentions labour codes. After 1952, labour laws 
were enacted to protect workers’ interests. Now, 29 labour laws have been scrapped 
and replaced by four labour codes. Labour is not exclusively in the Union List or the 
State List—it is in the Concurrent List. Our State opposed this. Tamil Nadu opposed 
one of the codes. Yet they were forced through by brute power. 
Sir, the national floor-level minimum wage of ₹176 was last revised in June 2017. 
Who stopped the Government from revising it? No one! Yet it has not been revised.

The Indian Labour Conference has not been held for 10 years. No initiative has been 
taken. Instead, attempts are being made to strip workers of their rights. In provident 
fund—both employee’s and employer’s contributions are cut. Sir, I will give an 
example. Though the budget made no mention of tea gardens, there are tea estates in 
our State under the Central Government. Andrew Yule is a Central Government 
company. Under it, four tea gardens—Karbala, Banarhat, Chunabhatti, and New 
Dooars exist. Over the last one and a half years, nearly ₹10 crore in PF dues have not 
been deposited for workers. This has been admitted by the Government in a written 
reply in this House.  We are also seeing a conspiracy to destroy our State’s jute 
industry. Instead of jute bags, plastic and poly-jute bags are being permitted for food 
grain storage in FCI godowns. These things are happening continuously. 
 Sir, I want to mention that our State Government has introduced a free social 
security scheme for unorganised workers—BMSSY, which isn’t otherwise available 
anywhere else in the country. One only needs to enrol. The Government deposits ₹55 
per month. At the age of 60, unorganised workers—who otherwise have no provident 
fund—receive benefits up to ₹2.72 lakh from our Government. Already, 1 crore 86 lakh 
unorganised workers have enrolled, 37 lakh have received benefits, and ₹2,910 crore 
has been disbursed. This is the alternative model. 
The Press Council of India, a statutory quasi judicial body to safeguard the Press 
freedom has been without a Chairperson for the first time in the history of independent 
India. The term of the Fourteenth Council ended more than a year ago, but the full 
Fifteenth Council is yet to be formed and the post of Chairperson has not been filled, 
while the last Chairman’s three-year extended term ended in December, 2025.  It is a 
very serious matter that the Government needs to address.

Nobel laureate Indian economist Abhijit Banerjee says: “India is turning into a mystery 
for the world not because of complexity but because the Government’s claims no 
longer match ground reality.  Headline GDP growth is being used as a shield to deflect 
attention from unemployment, poor education outcomes and widening inequality.

inconvenient numbers disappear and trust in governance collapses.  Media freedom is 
shrinking, leaving citizens without reliable scrutiny of power and investors without 
confidence in official narratives.  Extreme polarization has replaced policy thinking,

performance.  GDP can be managed; trust cannot. 
 *I want to quote Swami Vivekananda. In 1893, Swami Vivekananda, while 
speaking at the World Parliament of Religions in Chicago said, “if anybody dreams of 
the exclusive survival of his own religion and the destruction of the others, I pity him 
from the bottom of my heart, and point out to him that upon the banner of every religion 
will soon be written, in spite of resistance: "Help and not Fight," "Assimilation and not 
Destruction," "Harmony and Peace and not Dissension. ”  I will again invoke Nehru, 
Sir.  Nehru gave the very evocative example of the palimpsest.  What is a palimpsest?  
In ancient times, before the discovery or invention of paper, the palimpsest was either 
a tablet of stone or a tablet of wood on which every victor would erase the past history 
and write his own version of history.  But, in the Discovery of India, Nehru says, “India 
is an ancient palimpsest on which layer upon layer of thought and reverie had been 
inscribed, and, yet, no succeeding layer has completely hidden or erased what has 
been written previously.”  Sitting in Bengal, many dream of capturing Bengal. We have 
a different meaning of Bengal. To us, Bengal means Asia’s first college—Hindu

College, today’s Presidency University; Asia’s first university—Calcutta University;

Awaken slowly, O land of golden hearts, 
On this sacred pilgrimage of India, 
On the shores of the great ocean of humanity. 
Here I stand, stretching out both my arms, 
I bow in reverence to gods and to humans alike; 
In generous rhythm, in supreme bliss, 
I offer my salutations to them. 
Meditative and grave stand the mountains, 
Rivers move like rosary beads in prayer; 
Across the wide plains, every day I behold 
This holy earth of India. 
On this shore of India’s vast ocean of humanity, 
No one knows at whose call 
So many streams of people arrived— 
From where they came, in irresistible currents, 
Only to lose themselves in the sea.

Here the Dravidian and the Chinese, 
The Scythian, the Pathan, the Mughal— 
All were absorbed into one single body.

All who came—

None is distant, none is alien.

Their diverse melodies continue to sound;

On this shore of India’s great ocean of humanity.

SHRI GOLLA BABURAO (Andhra Pradesh):  Mr. Deputy Chairman, Sir, as I speak on 
the Motion of Thanks on the President’s Address today, I wish to thank first my leader 
and President of the YSRCP, Shri Jagan Mohan Reddy Garu, and the leader of my 
Party, Shri Subba Reddy Garu, for giving me this opportunity.  Before I go into the 
details, I really appreciate and congratulate the Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi, on 
the achievements made during these ten years.  For example, our economy is today 
the fourth largest economy in the entire world.  This is really not an ordinary 
achievement.  We should all congratulate the hon. Prime Minister on that.   
Coming to the important highlights, in India, the rate of poverty riddance is very, 
very rampant.  Around 25 crores of people have been brought above the poverty line in 
these five-ten years.  This is also a very big achievement.  Five crore pakka houses 
have been built for the poor people.  Twelve crore people have been given pipeline 
connection and lakhs of crores have been distributed to the poor people under the 
direct benefit scheme.  Such achievements are really going to achieve the goal of Viksit 
Bharat by 2047.  I have a lot of hope on the achievements made on every front in this 
regard.   
Coming to my own State of Andhra Pradesh, as the entire House knows, my 
State was divided in 2014 without any background or any guarantee.  We have gone 
100 years back as far as development is concerned.  Sir, I want to bring some important 
issues before this House.  I want to take the example of the Polavaram Project.  This 
Project is really a dream Project not only for Andhra Pradesh but for Telangana and so 
many other States also.  But, unfortunately, the Government of India has not given 
adequate funds till today.  In the last Budget, which was presented just two-three days 
ago, only Rs. 3,320 crores have been allocated for this project, which is a peanut.  
..(Interruptions).. Yes, you are correct, it is even less than a peanut.  Sir, I request the 
Union Government to at least see the dreams of the people of Andhra Pradesh.  As you 
all know, unlike other States, in Andhra Pradesh, three-engine train is going on, one is 
BJP, the second one is Telugu Desam Party and the third one is the Janasena Party.  
These parties have given a lot of promises to the people before coming to power.  I 
really cannot imagine that not even a single promise is fulfilled to get rid of the problems 
faced by the people of Andhra Pradesh.  The height of the Polavaram dam is also going 
to be reduced from 45 metres to 41 metres.  If it is reduced, the entire area will be

the Government’s voice sounded good, but we could not find the voice of Odisha. I will 
place all these things before you in the next six to seven minutes of my speech.  In the 
Address, there was no mention of the Federal Structure anywhere. Had the federal 
structure been mentioned, the Mahanadi issue would have been raised—where 
Chhattisgarh has built barrages upstream and is blocking the water of the Mahanadi 
from reaching Odisha. Despite a "Triple Engine" Government running in Chhattisgarh, 
Odisha, and Delhi, the Mahanadi issue was nowhere to be found. Had there been a 
mention of the Polavaram issue, the voice of Odisha would have been heard but why 
can’t the Triple Engine government of Andhra Pradesh, Odisha, and Delhi failing in 
providing justice to Odisha? 
Questions arise regarding the federal structure. If the federal structure was a 
priority, the demand for Special Category Status for Odisha, which our leader Shri

Bengaluru, Chennai, Delhi, Varanasi, and Siliguri—where is Odisha? 
 Sir, industrial corridors were given. Where? From Durgapur, the Eastern 
Corridor was given; a Dedicated Freight Corridor was given; ship repairing was given 
to Dankuni in Surat; ship repairing was given to Varanasi and Patna. Why not Odisha? 
Our Upper Coastal Odisha, Paradeep, Dhamra—why were they not included? 
'Purvoday' exists, but not for Odisha. This Address is not for Odisha. "Voice of Odisha 
is voice of government". 
Coming to Point number 16: in the last 11 years, 7,200 km of National Highways 
were built in the North East. It is good. But for the Odisha Coastal Highway, you have 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
101

 
 
been making declarations for ten years but not even a single brick has been laid—
please clarify that.  Point number 17 mentions the Dharti Aaba Janjatiya Gram Utkarsh 
Abhiyan for tribal brothers and sisters. I welcome and accept this good work. But why 
is the PESA Act not being implemented for Odisha's tribal brothers and sisters? Why 
are Gram Sabhas not being held where there is no PESA? Why are people being 
separated from their land? The PESA law is being violated. Everyone is silent on 
including Mundari, Bhumij, Ho, and Kui languages in the 8th Schedule. Remove GST 
from Tendu leaves for our tribal brothers and sisters—but the Government is silent. 
"Voice of Odisha is voice of Government". 
Point number 18 says 4 lakh crore rupees were given under PM-Kisan Samman 
Nidhi. This is a beautiful thing, I welcome it. But in the Mandis today, the rice of the 
Odisha farmer is not being lifted. Ten kilos are being cut (Katni-Chhatni). Farmers are 
committing suicide. This is the major issue for Odisha. "Voice of Odisha is voice of 
Government". 
 Point 23 says 3 crore women will be made 'Lakhpati Didis'. I welcome this. Every 
mother and sister should become a Lakhpati Didi to empower Odisha and the nation. 
But why are our mothers and sisters unsafe in Odisha today? Every day you see 
national headlines—someone setting themselves on fire with petrol in Balasore, a gang 
rape in Gopalpur—why is this happening? This is the stark reality of Odisha. "Voice of 
Odisha is voice of government". 
Point 35 says the Government has invested 50 lakh crore rupees in the last few 
years. Very good. But Odisha’s Coal Royalty hasn't been increased for the last ten 
years. We have lost 10,000 crore rupees because of this. There isn't a single word on 
this.   Sir, there are many issues to raise. The Address has beautiful topics. Hon. 
President Madam is the pride of Odisha; we voted for her and made her victorious. But 
in this Address today, where is Odisha? Today, 4.5 crore people of Odisha are asking; 
mothers and sisters are asking—did Odisha get anything, or did only the Government 
get something?  In this Address, there is a Government, but no Odisha. There is a 
Government, but no 4.5 crore people of Odisha. This is the reality. Thank you very 
much.

MR. DEPUTY CHAIRMAN : Now, Shri I.S. Inbadurai.  Maiden Speech in Tamil; ten 
minutes. 
SHRI I. S. INBADURAI: Mr. Deputy Chairman, Sir, this is my maiden speech.  You have 
to protect me.  I want to speak in my mother tongue Tamil for better understanding of 
my people.  
MR. DEPUTY CHAIRMAN:  Please speak.  
SHRI I.S. INBADURAI (Tamil Nadu):  Hon. Chairman Sir, I thank you very much for 
giving me this opportunity to express my views in my mother tongue, Tamil.  
“O, Prosperous Tamil! 
Sweet you are, like Honey! 
I have offered you my actions and my very breath! 
If you decline, my life too will decline. 
If your welfare is ensured, then mine is also ensured.” 
In this august House, where many distinguished leaders have served, I stand 
today with a sense of awe and deep emotion. I have come from Navalladi village, the 
southernmost village of India, a village where the salty sea breeze blows. Hailing from 
that remote village, I stand in this august House. This House has been graced by our 
ideological leader, Perarignar Anna, and today is his death anniversary. This is also the 
 
 English translation of the original speech delivered in Tamil. 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
105

 
 
House where our revolutionary leader Puratchi Thalaivi Amma Ms.  Jayalalithaa, who 
guided us in politics, has also served. Our leader, Mr. Edappadi K. Palaniswami,  has 
also served in this Parliament. It is he who has sent me to this House. At present, I am 
participating in the discussion on the Motion of Thanks on the hon. President’s 
Address. 
When I see the hon. President of India, I stand with pride that India has elevated 
a woman from a tribal community to the position of the first citizen of the country. This, 
Sir, is Dravidian ideology. Dravidam is not merely a word. It is not merely a noun.  It is 
an action-oriented programme for the upliftment of the downtrodden. Sir, that is 
Dravidam. Dravidian ideology stands for the empowerment of people at the grassroots.  
Today, Smt. Droupadi Murmu occupies the highest constitutional office of the country. 
Do you know who pioneered such empowerment?  When Shri Chaudhary Charan 
Singh was the Prime Minister of India, an opportunity arose for Tamil Nadu to have 
representation in the Union Cabinet. It was our leader, Puratchi Thalaivar  M.G. 
Ramachandran, who ensured that a woman from the Dalit community, Ms. Sathyavani 
Muthu, had to be  given that opportunity. Our party has worked for the upliftment of 
the downtrodden. In India, she was  the first Dalit woman to become a Union  Cabinet 
Minister. That historic achievement was made possible by the All India Anna Dravida 
Munnetra Kazhagam under the leadership of Puratchi Thalaivar M.G.R.  Subsequently, 
Puratchi Thalaivi Amma Ms. Jayalalithaa appointed Mr. Dhanapal , a leader from the 
Scheduled Caste community, as the Speaker of Legislative Assembly of Tamil Nadu. 
She elevated him to such a position that all the leaders had to bow to the authority of 
the Chair. All the leaders, irrespective of the leaders of the ruling party, leaders of the 
opposition party and Ministers had to pay reverence to the Speaker.  That was the 
hallmark of her leadership.  Justice Party was our ideological forerunner. Its objective 
was communal justice. That objective was translated into action by our Puratchi Thalaivi 
Amma. She secured 69 per cent reservation for Other Backward Classes, and ensured 
its implementation by placing it under the Ninth Schedule of the Constitution , and 
thereby guaranteeing constitutional protection for it.  Sir, in a General Constituency, 
anyone may contest elections. In a reserved constituency, only candidates from 
Scheduled Castes may contest. However, has any party fielded a Scheduled Caste 
candidate in a General Constituency and ensured their victory? It was Puratchi Thalaivi 
Amma Ms. Jayalalithaa who fielded Mr. Dalit Ezhilmalai in a General Constituency and 
106
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
 
ensured his success. This is Dravidam. Today, Mr. Edappadi Palaniswami stands as 
the torchbearer of that Dravidian legacy.  Adichanallur , located in my native region on 
the banks of the Thamirabarani River , is an important archaeological site that 
showcases the ancient civilisation of the Tamils. The Government of India, under the 
leadership of hon. Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi, has taken steps to develop 
Adichanallur and has allocated funds for this purpose. 
Sir, the India–European Union trade agreement has opened up new 
opportunities for the textile industries of Coimbatore and Tiruppur in Tamil Nadu. There 
may be ideological differences; however, I wish to place on record my appreciation for 
hon. Prime Minister Shri Narendra Modi for one significant achievement.  India was 
once mockingly referred to as a nation of snake charmers. Today, under the leadership 
of hon. Prime Minister Shri Narendra Modi, India has emerged as the fourth-largest 
economy in the world. For this achievement, I respectfully acknowledge and commend 
him. I would like to laud him in appreciative terms. A country that was once told to look 
up to Japan, France, and Russia has now reached a stage where Japan, Russia, and 
France stand behind India. This achievement deserves appreciation. And the credit 
goes to hon. Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi. India was once under British colonial 
rule. Today, Britain stands behind India in terms of economic strength. This is a 
development we must acknowledge and appreciate.  Just as India is progressing 
steadily on the path of development, Tamil Nadu too stands with pride. Yesterday, my 
esteemed colleague, Shri Siva, spoke in this House and stated that Tamil Nadu stands 
tall. If Tamil Nadu remains in the forefront today, it is because of our party AIADMK. Out 
of the last fifty years, the AIADMK has governed the State for thirty-one years , under 
the leadership of our leaders Puratchi thalaivar M.G.R, Puratchi thalaivi Amma Ms, 
Jayalalithaa, and Mr.  Edappadi Palaniswami.  As a result of this governance, while the 
National Education Policy aims to achieve 50 per cent Gross Enrolment Ratio in higher 
education by 2035, Tamil Nadu had already achieved 51.4 per cent fifteen years ago 
during the tenure of Mr. Edappadi Palaniswami.   Tamil Nadu has received the Krishi 
Karman Award for food grain production for four times. The State has also secured 140 
awards in local self-government. During the AIADMK Government, we had established 
11 medical colleges and 10 Universities in Tamil Nadu.  Sir, cooperation from the Union 
Government is essential for the progress of a State. We extend our hand for 
cooperation, but we raise our voice for our rights. 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
107

 
 
Sir, Tamil Nadu does not have metro rail projects in Madurai and Coimbatore. 
Upon examination, I came to know that the Detailed Project Reports were not 
submitted properly. I wish to state in this House that our party AIADMK is forming the 
Government in Tamil Nadu in three months. And we will submit fresh proposals and I 
request the Union Government to approve them. 
I wish to draw the attention of the House to another important matter. I was born 
and brought up in Kudankulam , where a nuclear power station operates. While 
electricity is generated for the entire nation, the people of Kudankulam themselves 
remain in darkness. The local population is not given priority in employment. This issue 
needs reconsideration.  Similarly, nearly eight lakh people are engaged in the Sivakasi 
fireworks industry, which is currently facing severe decline. It has been categorised 
under the Red Category. The authority has to reclassify it as White Category. This 
Parliament is empowered to enact the necessary legislation. Therefore, I request the 
Government to consider this issue. 
Sir, there is a direct train service from Kanyakumari to Kashmir , but there is no 
direct rail connectivity to Rameswaram, which is geographically closer. A new railway 
line of approximately forty kilometres, via Thisaiyanvilai , my native area, needs to be 
laid. Such a line existed during the British period but was later dismantled. I request 
that this line be restored. 
Sir, it is a matter of pride that a woman serves as the President of India. 
However, in the same country, women in Tamil Nadu do not enjoy adequate safety 
today.  There is a lack of safety for women, including women police personnel in Tamil 
Nadu. In Virukampakkam, a woman police personnel was subjected to sexual 
harassment. Journalists too are not safe; attacks on women journalists are taking 
place. People are questioning the status of law and order in Tamil  
Nadu. …(Interruptions)…

THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (SHRI TIRUCHI SIVA) :  Thank you, Ranjeet ji, you have 
concluded within your time.  I appreciate that.  Next speaker is hon. Member, Ms. Dola 
Sen, not present.  Next speaker is hon. Member, Dr. Vikramjit Singh Sahney, not 
present.  Next speaker is hon. Member, Shri S Niranjan Reddy.                                                             
SHRI S NIRANJAN REDDY (Andhra Pradesh): Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, I rise to reflect 
on the hon. President's Address. The Address reflects India’s confident assertion and 
transition into the second phase of 21 st Century with emphasis on technology-led 
128
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
 
growth, infrastructure expansion and inclusive development.  Sir, while I represent the 
State of Andhra Pradesh, this vision aligns with the State's dual character as a 
technology hub and agrarian coastal economy.  The hon. President's Address deals 
with all these aspects with sufficient details. I want to focus on one aspect that the hon. 
President's Address refers to, namely, the Government moving forward on the path of 
reforms express.  In the reforms’ express that the Government is embarked on, I find 
that there are certain other bogies that may need to be attached to this particular 
express. While various areas have been covered, I want to focus on two or three 
specific areas. The first one is on technology and digital economy. India’s progress in 
the digital economy is evident from the electronics production crossing rupees 11 lakh 
crore. Smartphone exports exceeding rupees one lakh crore in early 2025-26. Large-
scale AI skilling creates significant opportunities for Southern States like Andhra 
Pradesh and Telangana that host IT, GCCs, electronics and data center ecosystems. 
However, the expansion has exposed certain structural gaps. India's National 
Cybersecurity Policy dates back to 2013 which predates the scale of cloud computing, 
data centers, and AI-driven systems, critical digital public infrastructure, and 
underscoring the need for a modernized cybersecurity framework. At the same time, 
the industry estimates point to a substantial shortage of skilled professionals in 
emerging areas, such as cybersecurity, artificial intelligence, semiconductor design, 
and advanced electronics despite ongoing training initiatives. I note with satisfaction 
that the hon. President's Address refers to the necessity of increasing the skilling and 
also providing for education in these sectors from an earlier stage.  
Sir, the second area that I want to speak on, specifically, is with regard to the 
agricultural policy. So, these are the areas that, I think, largely remain, and while they 
have been covered in some other aspects, certain other aspects that have not been 
touched upon or something that I wanted to speak on.   I think there is a specific need 
for India to quickly adopt and move towards an agricultural policy.  We have a large 
percentage of our population still dependent on agriculture.  Agriculture’s contribution 
is increasingly reducing its share to the country's GDP, but the large extent of 
population continues to depend on agriculture. We find that in the agricultural policy 
that we have, while the Address refers complementarily to the increase in production 
of grains, I find that a lot of grains being produced are something that are even beyond 
the consumption capacity of the country and a lot of them are being diverted for 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
129

 
 
processing ethanol and also for other things.  I think, a cost-benefit analysis may need 
to be done with regard to the agricultural policy and we should have a long-term ‘land 
agricultural policy’ with regard to what would suit the country's interest best.  
Sir, then, women-led development also finds a part. We find, especially in South 
of India, population is aging faster. While this has been on the anvil of Government's 
developmental plan, we need to augment the workforce which can only be done very 
quickly if more women are brought into the workforce to add to the productivity level of 
the population. I find, while we have had some basic small schemes in relation to 
bringing women into this development of employment programmes, I think, something 
more needs to be done.   If India is to progress on the reforms’ express, one area that 
has not been touched upon is the necessity of judicial reforms.                                                       
Sir, we may have made some changes in relation to the penal laws. As I have 
been seeing the practice of those penal laws, I have not seen a great difference with 
regard to how it affected the actual performance of those laws in the country. We will 
need to make many more changes apart from that. While the process of decriminalizing 
is welcome, there are several judicial reforms that need to be brought in because if India 
is to move towards Viksit Bharat, we need to give an air of certainty in legal dealings to 
people who want to come into India or the businesses within India when they are dealing 
in business transactions. So what we find is that there is still a lot of hesitation with 
regard to contractual certainty in India. I think steps will need to be taken for the 
purpose of ensuring that we do something very quickly. Two very quick fix solutions 
are: we can make costs compulsory. When two parties litigate, one party benefits by 
delaying. We find that several people are going to court understanding that they do not 
have a case, but they know that they will be able to delay the matters in court. If we are 
able to come up with some mechanism which says that if someone is coming to the 
court without a good case or is dragging on the proceedings, like, it happens in most 
developing countries, we need to provide for compulsory costs which will be 
approximating to the actual cost apart from penalty. This will disincentivize  people to 
litigate, honour contracts, which will bring about certainty in the economy.  Also, 
another area with regard to providing and ensuring stability is that law and order would 
play a large part.  It is the Centre's responsibility to ensure that while States are 
operating the law and order in their independent spheres, if any particular State is 
130
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
 
transgressing this law and order situation and resulting in chaos, like, it is happening 
currently in the State of Andhra Pradesh, something would need to be done to ensure 
that this politicking at a State level does not disturb the economic stability and progress 
of the State.  From the State of Andhra Pradesh, I call upon the Central Government to 
look at the current law and order situation in the State of Andhra Pradesh and please 
bring it to order soon so that Andhra Pradesh can embark and continue on the growth 
journey. Thank you, Sir.             
THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (SHRI TIRUCHI SIVA): Thank you, Niranjan Reddy Garu. Next 
is Shrimati Sulata Deo.

THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (SHRI TIRUCHI SIVA ):  Thank you. Next speaker is Dr. 
Meenakshi Jain, not present. Now, hon. Member, Shri Gurwinder Singh Oberoi; you 
have two minutes to speak. 
SHRI GURWINDER SINGH OBEROI (Jammu and Kashmir):  Hon. Vice-Chairman, Sir, 
I rise to respond to the hon. President's Address.  But, I must state plainly that for 
Jammu and Kashmir, the Address is a study in contradiction.  It celebrates democracy 
while presiding over its systematic suspension in one part of the country.  Sir, Jammu 
and Kashmir today stands as the only region in India downgraded from a State to a 
Union Territory, stripped of its political agency and governed by Central command.  
While the President's Address speaks of federalism and cooperative governance, 
Jammu and Kashmir remains a living example of Centralized overreach and democratic 
rollback. The Jammu-Kashmir National Conference, a party that has defended the 
Indian Constitution for decades, refuses to accept this dangerous ‘normal’ of the 
abnormal.  You cannot dismantle representative institutions and then lecture this House 
on democratic strength.  Democracy cannot be suspended and celebrated at the same 
time. Hon. Vice-Chairman, Sir, the Address repeatedly invokes development.  But, let 
us be very clear, development without consent is not progress. It is an imposition. 
Roads cannot replace rights; investments and economic packages cannot compensate 
for the denial of dignity. If the situation in Jammu and Kashmir is truly normal, why is 
Statehood still withheld? If trust has been restored, why is power still centralized in 
Delhi? And, if this model is so successful, why is it not being applied anywhere else in 
India? 
Sir, the Address speaks of youth empowerment, yet the youth of Jammu and 
Kashmir face record unemployment, uncertainty and shrinking opportunities.  …(Time-

f~uJ, Oq^ c^US KS f~uh हjs  Hon. Modiji remains steadfastly committed to the welfare

Hon. Vice-Chairman Sir, Kashmir is rightly called Heaven on Earth, through 
which the Chenab River flows. Building a bridge over it posed a formidable challenge, 
yet an arch bridge now spans the Chenab, connecting Kashmir to the rest of India by 
rail. The Pamban Bridge links Mandapam with Rameshwaram and Dhanushkodi in 
Tamil Nadu; it is a vertical marvel successfully constructed by the Modi Government. I 
recall that work on a bridge over the Brahmaputra began in 1997-98, its completion 
long awaited. Revived in 2018 under hon. Prime Minister, Shri Narendrabhai Modi's 
directions, it was inaugurated on 25 December 2018—the longest railway-cum-road 
bridge, linking Dibrugarh and Dhemaji. 
Sir, the North-Eastern States were once poorly connected to mainland India. 
Modiji often says that if one organ of the body malfunctions, the whole body cannot be 
healthy; likewise, if one region lags, the nation cannot be developed. States like Assam, 
Mizoram, and Manipur were distant from the capital. With pride, I affirm they are now 
well connected via roads and railways. Enhanced connectivity with the Seven Sister 
States has spurred local development and boosted tourism, creating employment.  In 
earlier days, India had only five waterways; today, there are 100, with inland waterways 
flourishing. The Modi Government has achieved such herculean feats. The hon. 
President stressed infrastructure development in her Address, noting India's 
revolutionary progress. Over the past decade, infrastructure has advanced 
tremendously. India surges ahead in electronics and startups. Hon. Prime Minister Shri 
Narendrabhai Modi works tirelessly on rural development, women empowerment, 
labour law reforms, and more. 
Sir, as my time draws to a close, I conclude that the President's Address outlines 
the Government's direction, embodies strong nationalism, and charts the path ahead. 
Let us rise above party lines, placing the nation first, to build a Viksit Bharat, Strong 
Bharat, Prosperous Bharat, and Atmanirbhar Bharat. Jay Hind!”

SHRI G. K. VASAN (Tamil Nadu) : Sir, the President’s Address clearly outlines the

[THE VICE- CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S. PHANGNON KONYAK) in the Chair.] 
 Madam Vice-Chairman, I am equally happy to state that significant focus and 
attention on livestock and dairy development has been given in the Address.  India’s 
fisheries sector, especially, coastal and marine fisheries, has also witnessed 
unprecedented policy support.  I am sure that these measures will go a long way and 
they will be the stakeholders in India’s economic growth in the future.  Across all the

platforms have eliminated leakages and reduced corruption thereby ensuring that 
benefits reach the last mile with efficiency and dignity.   
 Madam, I would like to place particular emphasis on the President’s recognition 
of Tamil Nadu as a vital contributor to India’s economic and social development.  
Guided by the spirit of cooperative federalism, the Central Government has ensured 
sustained investment in Tamil Nadu across different sectors like National Highways, 
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
143

 
 
Railways, Ports, Logistics Infrastructure for enhanced Tamil Nadu’s connectivity and 
competitiveness, benefiting industry, agriculture and trade.  Tamil Nadu’s coastal 
economy has received focused attention through harbor modernization, export 
facilitation and welfare initiatives.  Agricultural diversification, dairy development and 
rural infrastructure support have strengthened the agrarian-based State of Tamil Nadu.  
At the same time, Tamil Nadu has emerged as a national leader in manufacturing, 
electronics, defence production, renewable energy, MSMEs, which are being 
supported by national initiatives, such as Production-Linked Incentive Scheme.  Skill 
development, digital infrastructure and start-up ecosystem have opened new avenues 
of jobs for the youth of the State.  This balanced growth in both urban and rural 
industrial and agrarian sectors reflects development and action.  
In conclusion, I would like to say that the President’s Address captures the 
essence of India’s journey rooted in reform, respectful of federalism, committed to 
security and confident in global engagement. It reflects a Government that delivers 
through system, through transparency and through unity.  With these words, I once 
again thank the hon. President for her Address and wholeheartedly support the Motion 
on behalf of my Party, Tamil Maanila Congress.  Thank you.  
THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S . PHANGNON KONYAK) :  Now, hon . 
Member, Shri Deepak Prakash; ten minutes.

SHRI NIRANJAN BISHI (Odisha):  Thank you, Vice Chairperson Madam. I offer my 
profound thanks for allowing me to speak on the Motion of Thanks to the hon. 
President's Address. Her Excellency, the President, is from our soil of Odisha; she is 
 
 English translation of the original speech delivered in Odia.  
[ 3 February, 2026 ]
147

 
 
our pride and glory. She belongs to our tribal community. We, the 15 crore tribal people 
of the tribal society in India, are very proud.  But Sir, currently, the tribal people in India 
are not safe. There are attacks on the land, water, and forests of the tribals. There are 
attacks on the education, health, socio-economic, and cultural rights of the tribals, as 
well as on their constitutional rights. Our ANWESHA scheme, our UGC, our 
scholarships, our stipends, our reservation in appointments, reservation in promotions, 
and reservation in political posts—everything is being diluted. The Atrocities Act is 
being diluted. Reservation is being gradually diluted. Recently, our hon. Union 
Education Minister had brought a very good system and a very good law through the 
UGC for the protection of our ST, SC, OBC, and women; India welcomed it, but it was 
stayed through various means. 
  Madam, in our universities, there are no ST or SC lecturers, readers, professors, 
or associate professors; hundreds of posts are lying vacant. It is necessary that the 
UGC implements measures to fill them. On the other hand, posts for ST and SC remain 
vacant in the High Courts and the Supreme Court as well. The Government should fill 
those posts through a Special Recruitment Drive; the Her Excellency the President 
should have mentioned this in her Address.  Madam, in Odisha, the former Chief 
Minister Shri Naveen Patnaik had initiated a beautiful scheme called the 'Anvesha' 
Urban Education scheme. Under this, ST and SC children could study the CBSE 
pattern course completely free of charge. That has been closed since January 25th; it 
remains closed even on January 26th.  Naveen Babu had established Odisha Adarsha 
Vidyalayas in 314 blocks and had started the NMMS scholarship scheme providing a 
stipend of 12,000 rupees. In those schools, ST, SC, rural OBC, and General students 
all studied. The admission which was from Standard 1 to Plus Two, that too is being 
closed. So, instead of things doubling in the 'Double Engine' Government, they are 
being shut down. Her Excellency said nothing on this matter. 
THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S . PHANGNON KONYAK ):  Kindly  
conclude.  …(Interruptions)…   
SHRI NIRANJAN BISHI: Furthermore, we had a beautiful scheme for health services 
in Odisha for ST, SC women, the poor, and farmers called the Biju Swasthya Kalyan 
 
 English translation of the original speech delivered in Odia.  
148
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
 
Yojana, started by Naveen Babu. There was a facility of 10 lakh rupees per year for 
women for health services and 5 lakh rupees for others. It provided free, high-quality 
health services in all hospitals. In this Ayushman Bharat, people have to pay money 
first, and then treatment happens. But the ST, SC, OBC, women, farmers, and the 
poor of Odisha do not have money; they cannot pay upfront and, therefore, cannot get 
treatment. Thousands of people are dying due to lack of medical care. Her Excellency 
mentioned nothing about this in the Address. 
Regarding the Eklavya Model Residential Schools, Madam, the Government has 
established only 400 Eklavya Model Residential Schools in India. However, we need 
3,000 Eklavya Model Residential Schools. Ironically, so far, the Government has only 
built 400 instead of 3,000, for that, I think the 'Double Engine' Government should have 
doubled their efforts rather than troubling.
